From g7, the knight attacks: f5, h5, e8, e6, f9...
It would attack the rook at e8, but not at g8.

So h8g8 seems like the best move. It saves the rook from the immediate capture by the bishop and the potential capture by the knight.

Let me just check if there are any other options.
What about the king moves?
c8d7: Rook at h8 is still captured by Bishop at g7 (Bxh8).
c8b7: Rook at h8 is still captured by Bishop at g7 (Bxh8).
Pawn moves: Rook at h8 is still captured by Bishop at g7 (Bxh8).

So the ONLY move that doesn't lose the rook immediately is h8g8 (or h8e8, but h8e8 loses to Nc7+).

Check again: Rook at h8, Bishop at g7.
Is the bishop actually attacking the rook?
Diagonal from g7: f8, h8.
Yes, g7 to h8 is a diagonal move. (g+1, 7+1) = (h, 8).
So the bishop at g7 is indeed attacking the rook at h8.

Therefore, the rook must move.
Safe squares for the rook:
- g8: Not attacked by Bishop(g7), Knight(e6), or Queen(f4).
- e8: Not attacked by Bishop(g7), Knight(e6), or Queen(f4). BUT attacked by Knight(c7) if it moves there.
- f8: Attacked by Knight(e6).
- d8: Attacked by Knight(e6).

Wait, what if the rook moves to g8, and then White plays Nc7+?
Black King is at c8. White Knight moves to c7.
This is check.
Black must move the king.
Suppose Black moves to b7.
Now the rook is at g8, the knight is at c7, the king is at b7.
Is the rook at g8 under attack?
Knight at c7 attacks: a8, a6, b5, d5, e6, e8.
No, g8 is safe.
Queen at f4 attacks: (already checked), not g8.
Bishop at g7 attacks: (f8, h8), not g8.

So h8g8 is the most resilient move here.

Wait, let me re-calculate the knight move e6 to c7.
e is 5th file, 6 is 6th rank.
c is 3rd file, 7 is 7th rank.
(5-3)^2 + (6-7)^2 = 2^2 + 1^2 = 4 + 1 = 5.
A knight move always has a distance squared of 5.
So yes, e6 to c7 is a legal knight move.

And c8 to c7?
c is 3rd file, 8 is 8th rank.
c is 3rd file, 7 is 7th rank.
The king is at c8, the knight moves to c7.
That's a check! (The knight at c7 is adjacent to the king at c8).
Wait, the knight at c7 is not "adjacent" but it "attacks" the king at c8?
Knight from c7 attacks: a8, a6, b5, d5, e6, e8.
Wait! Knight from c7 does NOT attack c8!
Let's re-calculate.
c7: (3, 7)
c8: (3, 8)
Difference: (0, 1).
That's not a knight move.
A knight move from c7 would be:
(3+1, 7+2) = (4, 9)
(3-1, 7+2) = (2, 9)
(3+2, 7+1) = (5, 8) = e8
(3-2, 7+1) = (1, 8) = a8
(3+2, 7-1) = (5, 6) = e6
(3-2, 7-1) = (1, 6) = a6
(3+1, 7-2) = (4, 5) = d5
(3-1, 7-2) = (2, 5) = b5
So knight at c7 does NOT attack c8.
It is NOT a check.

My mistake. Let me re-evaluate the knight at e6.
Does it attack c8?
e6 to c8: (5-3)^2 + (6-8)^2 = 2^2 + 2^2 = 4 + 4 = 8.
No, that's not 5. So knight at e6 does not attack c8.

Does any other piece attack c8?
- Queen at f4: (6, 4) to (3, 8)
Diff: (-3, 4). Not a queen move.
- Rook at a6: (1, 6) to (3, 8). Not a rook move.
- Bishop at g7: (7, 7) to (3, 8). Diff: (-4, 1). Not a bishop move.

So the King at c8 is actually quite safe for the moment.

So, what are the threats?
1. The Bishop at g7 attacks the Rook at h8. ( immediate loss of material).
2. The Knight at e6 attacks d8 and f8.

If Black plays h8g8:
- Rook is safe from Bishop.
- Rook is safe from Knight.
- King is safe.

Then White's next moves could be:
- Nc7 (Not a check, but controls e8, a8, a6, etc.)
- Qd6 (Not a check, but puts pressure on c7, e7, etc.)
- Nd8 (Not a check, but puts pressure on b7, f7, etc.)

Let's look at the legal moves again: h8g8, h8f8, h8e8, h8d8, c8d7, c8b7, h7h6, g6g5, h7h5.
h8g8 is clearly the best move to save the rook and minimize immediate loss.

Is there any reason to move the king instead?
If Black plays c8b7:
The Bishop at g7 captures the Rook at h8 (Bxh8).
That's a loss of a rook for nothing.

If Black plays any pawn move:
The Bishop at g7 captures the Rook at h8 (Bxh8).

If Black plays h8f8:
The Knight at e6 captures the Rook at f8 (Nxf8).

If Black plays h8d8:
The Knight at e6 captures the Rook at d8 (Nxd8).

If Black plays h8e8:
The Rook is safe from the Bishop (because it's now at e8).
The Rook is safe from the Knight at e6.
Wait, is it? e6 to e8 is not a knight move.
But then White might play Nc7.
As we saw, knight at c7 attacks e8!
So h8e8 is a move that saves the rook for one turn, but then it can be captured by Nc7.
But h8g8 is a move that saves the rook and doesn't put it in the path of a knight move to c7.

Wait, let me re-check the knight at c7 again.
Knight at c7 attacks: a8, a6, b5, d5, e6, e8.
Knight at e6 attacks: d8, f8, c7, g7, c5, g5, d4, f4.
So if Black plays h8e8, White plays Nc7 and takes the rook.
If Black plays h8g8, White plays Nc7 and the rook is still safe.

Therefore, h8g8 is the strongest move.
